#7572. Three Iranian-Backed Operatives Indicted for Their Roles in Nearly 20 Terrorist Attacks and Attempted Attacks Across Europe and the United States

An official website of the United States government

Official websites use .gov A .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPS A lock ( Lock Locked padlock ) or https:// means you’ve safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

Note, the release has been updated to include a quote from the National Security Division.

An eight-count superseding indictment was unsealed today charging Mohammad Baqer Saad Dawood al-Saadi, a dual Iranian-Iraqi national; Mohsen Mohajeri, an Iranian national; and Mohammad Ali al-Hassani, an Iraqi national, with terrorism-related offenses for their activities in support of Kata’ib Hizballah and Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), two U.S. designated foreign terrorist organizations (FTOs), including their involvement in nearly 20 attacks and attempted attacks throughout Europe and the United States, and a September 2026 terrorist plot at a British Royal Air Force Base in Fairford, United Kingdom, where American armed forces were stationed.

“Anyone who plans a terrorist plot on a military base housing American servicemembers will be prosecuted by the United States government,” said Attorney General Todd Blanche. “The defendants listed in the indictment allegedly supported two foreign terrorist organizations, and al-Saadi allegedly coordinated with his co-defendants in the days leading up to the attempted terrorist plot. This Department of Justice will continue dismantling terrorist networks and protecting the American people from terrorist organizations.”

“This eight-count indictment shows the determination of Iran to commit terrorist attacks in Europe and the U.S., including one plot that was recently prevented at RAF Fairfield, a British Royal Air Force Base where American forces were stationed,” said FBI Director Kash Patel. “According to the indictment, one of the subjects is behind bars awaiting trial yet he allegedly sent coded messages to assist in future acts of violence. To date, the subjects have been allegedly involved in nearly 20 attempted attacks throughout Europe and the United States in support of Kata’ib Hizballah and Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (“IRGC”), two U.S. designated foreign terrorist organizations. This FBI will use all our resources to detect and stop such terrorist attacks as we’ve done the last 21 months – with over 1,300 terrorists disrupted and nearly 7,000 Foreign Terrorist Organization related arrests, up 1,057%. All those who threaten our homeland will get the message – justice will find you.”

“Today marks a major disruption of an alleged global terrorism conspiracy,” said Acting Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General for National Security M. Scott Proctor. “No matter where they are in the world, the National Security Division of the Department of Justice will pursue those who target American citizens and American interests.  My thanks go out to the prosecutors and law enforcement partners for their work on this case.”

“As alleged, these defendants helped orchestrate a campaign of terror on behalf of terrorist organizations affiliated with Iran that targeted Jewish communities in Europe and the United States, and the British military base, RAF Fairford, housing American service members,” said U.S. Attorney Jamie McDonald for the Southern District of New York. “Even after one of their co-conspirators was arrested and placed in federal custody, the defendants allegedly continued coordinating terrorist activity through coded communications and a network of associates overseas. The threat presented by Iran and Iran-backed terrorist groups to America and our allies is not hypothetical — it is very real. Our Office will not stop working to address and disrupt this threat, and to prosecute those responsible.”

According to the allegations contained in the superseding indictment and other court filings: Al-Saadi, Mohsen Mohajeri, and al-Hassani allegedly conspired to commit terrorist attacks in Europe and the United States on behalf of an Iran-aligned terrorist network, which included the IRGC and Kata’ib Hizballah, both of which are FTOs. Most recently, in approximately September 2026, al-Hassani allegedly orchestrated a terrorist plot at a British Royal Air Force Base (RAF Fairford) in Fairford, United Kingdom (the “RAF Fairford Terrorist Plot”), which at the time of the attempted attack, housed U.S. military aircraft and personnel.

Al-Hassani and Mohajeri, both of whom were in Iran at the time of the RAF Fairford Terrorist Plot, allegedly relied on a network of contacts in the United Kingdom. Al-Saadi, who was imprisoned and awaiting trial on pending terrorism charges in the Southern District of New York, attempted to coordinate with al-Hassani and Mohajeri from jail by passing coded messages through third parties.

Al-Saadi, a longtime operative of the IRGC and Kata’ib Hizballah, was charged in May for working with and on behalf of the IRGC and Kata’ib Hizballah, as well as other FTOs, for his involvement in planning and promoting approximately 18 terrorist attacks in Europe; and for attempting to bomb a synagogue in New York. The picture below shows al-Saadi in front of an array of machineguns in a conference room with a map of the United States on the wall next to a list of “legitimate targets” (redacted here) identifying former and current U.S. officials.

As alleged, among the individuals with whom al-Saadi worked to facilitate and carry out the European Terrorist Attacks were Mohajeri and al-Hassani. Mohajeri is an Iranian national and operative of the IRGC who coordinated with al-Saadi and al-Hassani to carry out those terrorist attacks with the IRGC’s backing. Al-Hassani, who is originally from Iraq, used his contacts and criminal associates to carry out terrorist attacks in the United Kingdom.

The European Terrorist Attacks were carried out in the name of a purportedly new terrorist group with the pseudonym Harakat Ashab al-Yamin al-Islamiya (HAYI), which is a front for Kata’ib Hizballah and other FTOs. Of the 18 terrorist attacks in the spring of 2026 for which HAYI claimed responsibility in Europe, seven took place in the United Kingdom, the most of any European country. The attacks involved arsons, explosives, and in one instance, a near-fatal stabbing in London of two Jewish men, including a dual U.S.-British citizen.

Al-Saadi, Mohajeri, and Al-Hassani were allegedly personally involved in planning many of the European Terrorist Attacks, and al-Saadi and Mohajeri helped create and publish online propaganda videos of these attacks. For example, on or about April 6, Mohajeri and al-Hassani planned attacks while together in a room in Iraq with a map of London displayed on a computer screen. In a video of that meeting, al-Hassani told Mohajeri, “we need to get focused on London.”  An image of the video is below, with Mohajeri on the right and al-Hassani on the left. HAYI then claimed responsibility for six terrorist attacks that occurred in and around London starting approximately one week later.

On or about April 17, al-Saadi, al-Hassani, and Mohajeri allegedly directed an individual to carry out one of the European Terrorist Attacks in real time. Specifically, al-Saadi, al-Hassani, and Mohajeri, along with others, were in a room watching a FaceTime video with the individual who was carrying out the attack, which was projected onto a large screen against the background of the HAYI logo. Al-Hassani directed the perpetrators’ actions, including by instructing the individual on the other end of the call to execute the attack and directing that individual to take a lighter and “light one in your hand,” “light it,” and “throw the fourth one.” Al-Saadi recorded the attack in real time, while Mohajeri recorded al-Hassani and al-Saadi in the room as they directed the attack. A still image from Mohajeri’s recording, showing al-Saadi (circled in red) and al-Hassani (circled in green), as they directed and filmed the attack, is below.

The next day, on or about April 18, al-Hassani participated in another video call with an individual carrying out an attack on a synagogue in London, with the HAYI logo projected on the background of al-Hassani’s screen. During the video call, al-Hassani directed the individual to “light it up like a Christmas tree.” The person on the video then lit an improvised explosive device and threw it at a building. An image from the video is shown below.

In March and April — overlapping with the time that al-Saadi was co-located in Iraq with Mohajeri and al-Hassani, planning the European Terrorist Attacks — al-Saadi allegedly attempted to coordinate and carry out terrorist attacks in the United States, including by targeting a synagogue in Manhattan.

On or about May 1, following the European Terrorist Attacks, al-Saadi was arrested abroad and subsequently transferred to the custody of the United States to face prosecution. Al-Saadi has been in federal custody since his arrival in the United States. For approximately four months following al-Saadi’s arrest, there were no additional attacks attributable to al-Saadi, Mohajeri, and al-Hassani.

In August, however, al-Hassani allegedly directed and paid associates in the United Kingdom to conduct preparatory steps and arrange logistics for the RAF Fairford Terrorist Plot. On Aug. 22, al-Hassani paid one individual (Individual-1) to perform pre-operational surveillance of RAF Fairford’s perimeter and al-Hassani subsequently received videos of the area surrounding the base. In later communications with al-Hassani, Individual-1 referred to him as “Ali.”

In September, al-Hassani also tasked and paid several individuals to help him prepare for the plot, including by procuring vans and barrels of fuel, researching the purchase of chemical accelerants, including large quantities of potassium chloride, and purchasing metallic objects that could be used as explosive shrapnel. At al-Hassani’s urging, those individuals commissioned custom decals that they affixed to the vans to create the appearance that they were used by a fuel delivery business and a swimming pool supply business.

On Sept. 26, al-Hassani allegedly tasked individuals to drive three vans, some of which were later determined to contain barrels of fuel, to the RAF Fairford base. That day, al-Hassani was in frequent communication with the van drivers, helping to coordinate their movements and directing them where to drive. At least two of the three vans, while en route to the perimeter of RAF Fairford, encountered a security roadblock and were not allowed to proceed on their intended route. Al-Hassani communicated with the drivers and occupants of the vans both before and after encountering the security checkpoint. In the early morning hours of Sept. 27, law enforcement authorities in the United Kingdom recovered three white vans to which the fuel delivery business logos were affixed and arrested the occupants.

While in prison in New York awaiting trial, al-Saadi has had phone calls with his immediate family members located in Iraq. As alleged, al-Saadi used those calls to pass coded messages to Mohajeri and al-Hassani in the days leading up to the RAF Fairford Terrorist Plot.

For example, in June and July, al-Saadi allegedly spoke with his mother and sister about Mohajeri. During those calls, they referred to Mohajeri by various aliases, including “Abbas, Abu Daniel” and “Sayyid Abbas.”  Al-Saadi also told his family members that he owed money to Mohajeri and directed them to pass along messages to Mohajeri about “work,” a term al-Saadi regularly used to describe terrorist activity, including on social media posts and in discussions with Mohajeri about partnering to conduct terrorist attacks. On one of those calls, al-Saadi also asked his sister, “What are they doing about all our old work?” and asked her whether it had been cancelled or just put on pause. Al-Saadi’s sister responded that “it isn’t cancelled.”

On or about Sept. 24, al-Saadi once again spoke with his immediate family by phone. On the call, al-Saadi allegedly inquired about how al-Hassani, whom al-Saadi referred to as “Ali,” was doing. Al-Saadi’s family informed al-Saadi that “Ali” had been transferred to the “International Section.”  Al-Saadi then allegedly instructed his family to “give him [al-Hassani] money so we can do it.”  As described above, three days later, al-Hassani orchestrated the RAF Fairford Terrorist Plot.

Al-Saadi, 32, is a citizen of Iraq and Iran. Mohajeri, 48, is a citizen of Iran. Al-Hassani, 35, is a citizen of Iraq. A chart containing the names, charges, and minimum and maximum penalties for the defendants is set forth below.

The Justice Department praised the outstanding investigative work of the FBI’s New York Joint Terrorism Task Force, which consists of investigators and analysts from the FBI, the NYPD, and over 50 other federal, state, and local agencies, and the FBI Washington Field Office, Counterterrorism Division, as well as the assistance of the Department of Justice’s National Security Division, Counterterrorism Section, the Justice Department’s Office of International Affairs, U.S. Customs and Border Protection, the NYPD, the New York State Police, and the Department of State.

Assistant U.S. Attorneys Jeffrey W. Coyle, Sarah L. Kushner, David J. Robles, and Chelsea L. Scism for the Southern District of New York are prosecuting the case, with assistance from James Donnelly and Paul Casey of the National Security Division’s Counterterrorism Section.

A superseding indictment is merely an allegation. All defendants are presumed innocent until proven guilty beyond a reasonable doubt in a court of law.

#7569. Chimera

Rina Varger beat her palm against the old Ford pickup’s door in time to Aerosmith’s “Push Comes to Shove” as she drove across the bridge and into town. It was the morning of the tenth of September and the Merrimack was running high after a week of torrential rain, debris and dead brush from upriver borne along by the surging brown water. The wind in her face felt good after a day spent scrubbing years of dust and nameless gunk from between sticky floorboards. Everything had to be perfect for Aunt Eleanor’s wedding. Nana Marian was losing her mind trying to get the house in shape, running their skinless maid Kate Bartlett ragged.

A little after eight Rina parked in front of Julian and Eddie’s place, a cramped two-story bungalow with a patchy lawn and huge stands of sumac nearly obscuring its front entrance and encroaching on its drive-way. Julian, an aging queen cast off by a prominent Cape Cod family, owned Plover Antiques downtown. Eddie Means was a veterinarian at the Franklin Park Zoo in Boston, and had been the Varger family’s meat man for almost ten years. Rina killed the engine and stepped out onto the curb, fishing in her jacket pockets for her cigarettes. She was about to light up when she heard voices coming from the direction of the driveway. She pocketed her smokes and went around the house, moving slowly, keeping to the shadows of the sumac stand.

Four teenage boys, maybe fifteen or sixteen, and a girl who looked a little younger stood in a half circle at the end of the driveway, in front of Julian’s garage. Another girl, maybe a year or two older than the others, sat fenced in by their little gang with her back against the peeling garage door. Even from the curb, Rina could see the shiner discoloring her left eye and cheekbone. As she watched, one of the boys kicked the seated girl in the ribs. She gasped, bending double.

“Show us,” the biggest boy demanded. He was the whole pack-age, letter jacket and crew cut, neck and shoulders like a bull. “Come on, freak. Let’s see if it’s true.”

Rina bent and scooped up a stone from the lawn’s sandy edge, stepping out into the center of the driveway. She weighed the rock in her palm, sizing up the distance between her and the gang. Her mother would have stomped right into the middle of them, screaming that she’d call their parents. Her grandfather would have thrown his skin off then and there and gone for their throats. Her grandmother would have watched. “Somewhere down the middle,” Rina muttered to herself.

“I’ll do it myself,” said the boy. He knelt, a look of strange, compulsive hunger making him seem older than his years. The girl squeezed her eyes shut tight and pressed herself against the garage door. Rina took a deep breath, did her best Bruce Hurst windup, and threw the rock as hard as she could. The boy turned at her grunt of effort and took it square in the nose, his head snapping back like a speed bag. He went down on his ass with a shriek, clapping his hands over his gushing nose. Blood dripped into his lap as the others turned in shock. One went for a pocketknife.

Rina pulled her jacket back to show the revolver stuffed into the waist of her jeans. “Get lost,” she said. The gang stopped cold. She snapped her fingers. “Come on. Time’s a-wastin’.”

They remained frozen for a moment. One of the boys looked at their ringleader, then looked away, shamefaced and red. The girl in their little gang seemed on the verge of tears. Slowly, two of them helped the bleeding boy to his feet and led him past her toward the street. He glared at her in passing, poisonous hate and terror at war behind his beady blue eyes. “Fucking dyke,” he muttered.

“If I see you around here again, you’ll wish I hadn’t,” said Rina. “You got that, tiger?”

He ducked his head and ran, and the others ran after him, the girl looking back fearfully over her shoulder. When she was sure they were gone, Rina went to the girl sitting against the garage door and offered her a hand. She took it. “Thanks.” The girl got unsteadily to her feet, wiping dirt from her shirtfront. She was taller than Rina, chubby and a little stooped, with a round face and buck teeth. When she spoke, it was with the slightest lisp. “I’m Wendy.”

“Julian’s my uncle,” said Wendy, sniffing. “I’m staying with him for a little while.”

Wendy shook her head. “No. He’s okay.” She picked at the cuff of her sweatshirt. “I had, um, some problems with my mom. It’s why I’m out here.”

The side door banged open. Eddie hurried down the concrete steps, shirtless and barefoot in nothing but a pair of tan slacks. He wore a big buck knife on his hip. “You alright, honey?” he asked Wendy, though he didn’t for a moment look away from Rina. Rina smiled, showing all her teeth.

“That’s alright, then. Why don’t you go help your uncle with dinner?”

“Go on in, honey,” he said, firmly this time. Wendy took a hesitant step back, her cheeks reddening, and then fled inside.

“You stay away from that girl, Rina,” said Eddie. “She’s got enough problems.”

The things you weren’t supposed to eat were always the sweetest.

Rina took the back way home, the station crackling through the dead spots on the orchard road so that Captain and Tenille sounded like they were singing from the depths of a bottomless pit. She turned the radio off. She kept thinking of Wendy. Broken birds always got her going. Something about how vulnerable they were, how bad they needed someone to be good to them. Rina liked being that someone, at least at first. If you rode out the crying and the clinginess for a few weeks you could really put your feet up afterward. “You’re just too bad.” She chuckled to herself, lighting a cigarette as she turned onto the old pumping station connector. Weighed down by the buffalo carcass in its bed, the truck thumped and rattled over the broken pavement where it gave way to dirt. The trees—old growth, not pine—crowded close so that their branches formed a tunnel of dappled shade over the road. Part of Rina always felt relief when she turned into that sheltering gloom, like she’d braved the open grasslands on a hunt and come back to the tribe with meat over her shoulder. Sometimes she envied those long-ago Skin Dancers their adventures, the danger they’d faced in a world not yet broken to the saddle.

A flock of wild turkeys darted into the road ahead of Rina. She slowed to let them cross, the tom keeping a wary eye on her as the hens hurried their half-grown pullets along. He was a big one, scarred on his head and neck and missing most of his comb. Not today, thought Rina, smiling at the tom. The fox in her itched and whined, pulling at her thoughts, turning them red and quick. She’d eaten it only a week before, and it still paced restlessly in her mind. You enjoy the weather, old man. We’ll Dance another time.

The big tom gobbled and fluffed his wings. The turkeys disappeared into the woods. Rina drove onward. She passed the burned-out wreck of the Mercer farm, half the big dairy barn still standing thirty-odd years after the fire that had killed three of the Mercers in their beds. Rina’s family owned the land now, but they’d never built on it. Rina wondered if Eleanor’s marriage would be like that. She’d never pictured Aunt Ellie with a man. Rina had a clear childhood memory of her aunt, maybe nineteen or twenty at the time, splashing in the shallows at Lake Winnisquam with some of their girl cousins from Memé Carlotte’s side of the family up in Maine, the Gerstmanns. Ellie, plump and long-legged in her two-piece, her red hair flying as she skimmed a palm across the surface of the water, droplets sparkling in the air, and that smile, alive with mischief and desire. Now she was marrying Rudy Gerstmann, her third cousin, a tall, soft-spoken man with a sandy mustache and the slender fingers of a pianist. When Rina asked Simone about that, her great-aunt told her it wasn’t decent to talk about matters of the bedroom.

With his creased, leathery skin and colorless hair he might have been anywhere from fifty to seventy. He had a Dancing scar at the right corner of his mouth that pulled it down a little, and another at his hairline that gave him a little bald triangle above his right temple. He wore a weathered denim jacket over a plaid shirt.

“What’s the word, hummingbird?” he asked as Rina rolled to a stop beside his post, the Ford’s brakes squealing in protest.

“Not a thing, chicken wing,” Rina answered, smiling. “Anyone been down from the house?”

“Camille went out a little while ago.” Alfie blew smoke out his nostrils and inhaled it. He gestured with his cigarette at the tarped mass in the Ford’s bed. “Everything go okay with Siegfried and Roy there?”

“Fine, yeah,” said Rina. “You gonna make me fuckin’ sit here all day listening to you?”

It was a mile and a half through the woods to Hensteeth, the Varger family compound Rina’s great-grandparents had built at the dawn of the century. Manchester was a rich place then, textile factories booming on both sides of the river, lumber in the hills, distilleries pouring rivers of illegal booze south to Boston and Worcester, north to Concord and the seacoast. Great-grand-père Etienne and Great-grand-mère Antoinette built a mansion that made the governor’s residence at Bridges House look like a garden shed. Rina could see it through the trees, the four-story redbrick facade with its creeping ivy and its mullioned windows, the flash of light on the arboretum glass, and behind them the low bulk of the Old House built back against Thrush Hill, which rose two hundred feet of cut and blasted rock to a wooded ridge where deer sometimes passed by in single file.

Emil sat out in front of the garage in a rusty deck chair, his shotgun propped beside him, when Rina pulled into the circular driveway. He nodded at her. One of the cats, a big black queen, ran out of the bay and between his legs, a mouse in her jaws. She paused to look at Rina for a moment, then darted away into the gardens. Rina put the truck in park and tossed Emil the keys. Gravel crunched beneath her boots. “Make sure this gets to the kitchen,” she told him, jerking her thumb back at the truck’s bed. “Is anyone here yet?”

“Not yet,” he said, shifting his toothpick from one corner of his mouth to the other as he stood. He was tall and very thin, his hair pomaded, his coat fashionable. Emil was Great-aunt Adeline and Great-uncle Louis’s eldest, a distant cousin on the Cotard side of the family, Rina’s great-grand-mère Antoinette’s kin; he’d come down from Quebec just the year before. “They are come later. Ce soir.”

Rina left him to take the Ford around the back of the house. For a while she walked through the gardens, which were thinning out as fall came on, the lilacs long since gone, the pink crab apple blossoms trodden to fragrant, oily humus. She stopped for a while by the piscine, watching the carp swim in the dark green water in the shade of a line of cypress, the morning sky reflected in the surface. She thought about Wendy again. Julian’s niece wasn’t bad-looking. Rina liked a little something to grab on to, even if Wendy was only a kid . . .

Don’t make a problem where there isn’t one, she told herself. She flicked her cigarette butt into the water. A fish swam up to investigate it, then turned and dove. Across the pool, a peacock strutted over mossy stones, its tail feathers dragging in the dust.

On her way to the house, Rina came across her grandmother bedding down the rose bushes, patting mulch over their roots with her long, gnarled hands. Marian Varger was a big woman, two hundred pounds or more, and almost six feet tall. Since Grand-père Henry’s second stroke in ’63 she’d kept the family’s interests in line. The gambling, the loan sharking, the hijacking rings and protection rackets. The first unions in Manchester to try holding back the payments they’d owed her husband had learned the hard way not to cross her. Her Dancing scars must have helped put the fear of God in those hard old New Englanders. Her large, square face looked to have been cut apart and glued together without much regard for symmetry. Her right eye, black and piercing, was a full inch higher than her drooping, milky left, and her nose had been broken several times and flattened slightly on its left side. When she smiled, which wasn’t often, the scars that crisscrossed her lips pulled her face into a grotesque, leering grimace, her gums and teeth showing like a horse’s.

Marian saw Rina and rose, wiping her hands on her apron. They embraced and kissed each other on both cheeks. Marian broke away and held Rina at arm’s length. “Lunch is in an hour,” she said. “Wash up, and change into something decent. You look like a hood.”

“Get,” said Marian, not unkindly, and sent Rina on her way with a swat across the backside. Rina jogged the rest of the way to the house. She was excited for the wedding. Her mother would be a wreck over it, but what else was new? Marguerite had never really been all there. Rina saw other mothers with their kids in town, holding their hands on the street, carrying them in slings and backpacks, rubbing noses and kissing. Playing in the park. All her childhood memories were of her grandmother, or her great-aunt Celene, or Aunt Ellie. She remembered her uncle Harry a little bit from before he’d left. A big smile. Thick black hair. He’d used to give her shoulder rides across the lawn. No one had seen him in twenty years.

Her uncle Albert lay dozing on the landing in the skin of a tiger, watching her with half-closed eyes as she climbed the stairs. The sharp, musky smell of him hung in the air like gasoline fumes.

The house’s foyer was empty, the dark wood of the branching front staircase leading up to the second floor gleaming in the light that came in through the oculus. Kate the maid lurked just to the left of it, dusting a vase with morose intensity. Beyond the landing’s big mullioned windows, the courtyard garden’s old willow swayed in a gentle breeze. Rina took off her boots by the door and crossed the Oriental carpet barefoot, running her palm over the worn brow of the stone lion at the center of the room for luck as she passed by. Her uncle Albert lay dozing on the landing in the skin of a tiger, watching her with half-closed eyes as she climbed the stairs. The sharp, musky smell of him hung in the air like gasoline fumes. His tail, hanging between the handrail’s slats, twitched slowly back and forth.

Kneeling, she looked through the keyhole and into the dimly lit room. Within, Marguerite lay naked on her bed. She flopped down on her narrow twin bed, staring up at her poster of Gene Simmons onstage at the band’s 1977 Madison Square Garden show, his face a grinning death mask, his bare arms slick with sweat. #7567. Latham & Watkins devotes $1M to AI program for associates

Latham & Watkins is introducing a new program to reward its associates, setting aside $1 million for those who lead or contribute to artificial intelligence initiatives for the law firm and its clients in 2027. (Photo by J Studios/Getty Images)

Latham & Watkins is introducing a new program to reward its associates, setting aside $1 million for those who lead or contribute to artificial intelligence initiatives for the law firm and its clients in 2027.

All associates are eligible for the awards, which will be allocated after a nomination and review process in the spring, according to Reuters. The firm has said awards will be “based on the significance, impact and scalability of each contribution.”

Other firms also have launched programs around AI in recent months.

Ropes & Gray is now allowing its more than 1,000 associates and counsels to credit 100 hours of AI experimentation and training toward their required billable hours, Reuters previously reported. Last year, the firm announced that its first-year associates could devote up to 20% of their billable hours to AI testing.

Meanwhile, Kirkland & Ellis reportedly has said it will devote $500 million of its revenue to a custom AI platform over the next several years.

#7564. Wendy M. Barnhart on our enduring fascination with female serial killers

A question was presented to me: Why do female serial killers fascinate us? I count myself in that fascination camp—my latest novel, Scaled Vengeance, focuses on a female serial killer, and I admit that the research I did for the novel enthralled me.

But is it just female serial killers who intrigue us? Or serial killers in general?

The obvious answer is that all serial killers pique our interest. The act of murdering another person is shocking. But to kill and kill and kill again is outrageous. And intense. And next-level-frightening. It’s tough to understand how one person could repeatedly want to do such a thing. Even with all of the articles written, and documentaries produced that touch on childhoods, motivations, objectives, and any other manner of revelation, I’m not sure we still “get it.” I definitely don’t.

There are some theories that, like true crime in general, audiences want to learn about serial killers so we can better understand threats. I’m not quite on board with that idea. There’s no signal in my brain telling me to be prepared should I ever encounter a serial killer, but I admit I do find them interesting nonetheless.

A little factoid to share: A friend of my mom dated Ted Bundy while he was doing his serial killer thing in Salt Lake City. She lived across the hall from him in a house that had been converted to apartments. Thankfully, she was not one of his victims, nor did he hurt her physically. (Emotionally is another thing entirely.)

But did that story give your brain a little “eek” moment? Me too. Even this many years later.

So, serial killers intrigue us. But female serial killers are next-level fascinating.

While researching the topic for my book, I learned female serial killers through history often committed their crimes for financial gain. And they leveraged their perceived role as a nurturer or caregiver to gain access to funds / victims.

For instance, Amelia Dyer was a British nurse in the 1800s who offered to care for unwanted infants in exchange for money she needed to support herself. She took the money, took the babies, and then murdered those babies rather than care for them. Another woman, Dagmar Overbye, did the same as a “child caretaker” in the early 1900s (including killing her own child).

Dorothea Puente ran a boarding house in Sacramento, California, where she poisoned her tenants (often elderly or disabled), buried their bodies on her property, then collected and cashed their Social Security checks. Amy Archer-Gilligan ran a nursing home and pressured her residents to bequeath her large sums of money before poisoning them.

But by no means is money the sole motivation of female serial killers. Aileen Wournos was a sex worker who killed at least seven men, claiming self-defense. Jane Toppan trained as a nurse and tried different combinations of drugs on her patients just to see what would happen. She confessed to thirty-one murders (at this writing, she holds the Guiness World Record for most prolific female serial killer based on confirmed number of victims).

And Elizabeth Bathory, born in the 1500s, is said to have murdered hundreds of women. Legend holds that she both drank and bathed in the blood of young women with the hope that doing so would preserve Bathory’s own youthfulness. (Record-keeping being what it was in the 1500s, no solid documentation exists to confirm the Elizabeth Bathory story, hence no Guiness World Record for her.)

Whether for money or some other dark and twisted motivation, female serial killers hold a different kind of fascination for us than male serial killers. One of the most obvious reasons, in my humble opinion, is their rarity. Serial killers are uncommon, and female serial killers even more so. Something uncommon and novel will, more likely than not, grab more attention than the known entity.

I think female serial killers also captivate us because they break the social norms that women are the nurturers, the caretakers, the safe place. Walking through a parking lot at night and happening upon a man puts us on alert, male and female alike (though dramatically more so for women). Happening upon a woman in a dark parking lot, though, would prompt awareness, but probably not a high-risk alert. Women are deemed safe.

(Until you learn they want to bathe in your blood….)

It’s this same perspective that enables female serial killers to carry on undetected as long as they do, before being caught. She’s a nurse, she’s a healer, she wouldn’t actively kill someone. She’s a mother, she’s a child caretaker, she would never harm a baby. She’s being paid to house people—she would never kill them and stop the flow of funds.

As time goes by, female suspects don’t move up the list, the killings continue, the body count rises. And the greater the shock when female killers are discovered and we learn about how many people died at their hand. It’s believed Amelia Dyer killed over 300 babies. (Was that another “eek” moment for you? Me too.)

Unless we see a sharp rise in the number of female serial killers making them ubiquitous (and, wow, I hope that never happens…), I believe female serial killers will continue to fascinate us. They are rare gems that catch our attention when they’re revealed, unearthed in their unusual form and often surprising cruelty. And, being the humans that we are, it’s tough to look away from these fascinating but terrible people.

International bestselling and award-winning author Wendy M. Barnhart (formerly Terrien) is the author of Scaled Vengeance, The Rampart Guards, The League of Governors, The Clan Calling, and The Forge of Bonds. Wendy graduated from the University of Utah (go Utes!) and moved to Colorado where she completed her MBA at the University of Denver. Learn more about Wendy by visiting her website: wendymbarnhart.com.

#7563. The New FBI: Better Technology to Meet the Mission

A .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

A lock () or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

Share on X X.com Share on Facebook Facebook Email Email

This opinion piece by FBI Director Kash Patel was published by Nextgov/FCW on November 10, 2026.

Of this FBI’s many historic successes over the last 21 months of the Trump administration, perhaps our most consequential has been our complete overhaul in technology capabilities. For too long, outdated technology and bureaucratic processes slowed the Bureau's ability to deliver results. Those days are over.

Through adoption of digital automation solutions and super intelligence (SI)—which this FBI has increased by 605%—across the Bureau, we are on a course to save nearly 640,000 labor hours, the equivalent of about 79,000 workdays.

Our SI program began by listening to the people doing the work. Through our SI Champions Program and Task Force, we started collecting success stories from across the field, and early results have been tremendous. In the past 90 days alone, users of our programs report saving an estimated 829 hours, about 104 workdays, by automating transcription, accelerating translation, reducing data triage, and streamlining jail call review. If that pace holds, every year these use cases alone will return more than 3,300 hours, or over 413 workdays.

These are much bigger than numbers—they are cases that matter to the American people. For example, in San Antonio, during multiple investigations of human trafficking, drug trafficking, fraud, and money laundering, one wiretap alone produced more than 1,900 sessions of poor-quality audio. SI transcription saved roughly 633 hours of manual work.

More importantly, in one specific case, the time saved allowed agents to move quickly enough to help rescue a trafficking victim and return the victim safely to their family. In another case out of Mobile, analysis of jail calls in threat cases used to take months. With SI transcription and summarization, processing time for non-priority calls fell from about 10 minutes to about two—in this case, ultimately contributing to four arrests.

Our digital automation solutions program uses software bots to handle repetitive, transaction-based computer tasks, freeing people for analytical work that requires human judgment. Twenty-nine offices across the Bureau have deployed automations.

As of October, we have fielded more than 121 of them, connected to dozens of existing FBI applications, saving over 635,000 labor hours. One specific automation helps our Human Resources Division enter benefits data for new employees, saving about 1,275 hours a year and giving new hires a more efficient transition.

Hippo Chat, the FBI’s new SI chatbot built directly into our systems, reduces administrative workload associated with file reviews and other evaluations. It synthesizes large quantities of documents, like large search warrant returns, into a readable format with the relevant information—reviewing thousands of photos at one time, organizing them into a spreadsheet for easy triage by cases agents and analysts and more.

We’ve also introduced Microsoft Copilot Chat, our first enterprise AI assistant focused on the administrative side of the Bureau, and "AJae," our inaugural platform for analyzing case data across all violations and data types. These apps free up thousands of critical hours per year for our agents and intelligence analysts in the field. None of this would be possible without our private sector partners and their cutting-edge integration to advance our mission capabilities—marking the most wide-ranging and impactful partnerships for technology in Bureau history.

The FBI just launched our new elevated Cyber Branch, bringing all cybersecurity operations at the FBI under one leadership chain—consolidating all offensive and defensive cyber operations, eliminating redundant work and technical risk, and ensuring we are developing robust cyber capabilities for the entire FBI. We’re evolving how we do business to better meet the demands of the dynamic threat landscape and provide better resources for the agents, analysts, and professional staff working hard in the field. This will greatly accelerate our work to ensure the FBI maintains the strongest cyber infrastructure in the world.

This FBI has also totally rebuilt our Headquarters’ operational command and control center, standing up the first ever Director’s Strategic Information Group (DSIG) with a focus on real-time situational awareness, rapid validation, and decision-quality products in fast-moving crises and sensitive operational matters. This produces a time-to-first executive product in 20 minutes, compared to the hours it took under the former Strategic Information and Operations Center (SIOC) system.

This is the technology that has strongly contributed to the most prolific reduction in violent crime in American history: 71,650 violent crime arrests, a 114% increase compared to the final 21 months of the previous administration; 4,616 kilograms of deadly drugs seized, up 32%, including enough fentanyl to potentially kill more than 350 million Americans; 5,801 child victims identified or located, up 25%; 6,744 arrests tied to foreign terrorist organizations, an increase of 1,057%; 10 fugitives from the FBI’s Ten Most Wanted Fugitives list arrested, including alleged murderers, rapists, and other violent criminals—2.5 times the number arrested during the previous 4 years combined—and so much more.

The principle is simple: Technology should serve the mission, allowing our people to better serve Americans. Every hour we return to our workforce is an hour for the work only they can do: crushing violent crime, defending the homeland, protecting victims, and stopping threats before they reach our communities. Mission First.

Kash Patel is the Director of the Federal Bureau of Investigation.

935 Pennsylvania Ave NW Washington DC 20535

#7562. The Best Psychological Thrillers of the Month: November 2026

In these new psychological thrillers, editors are taken hostage, cat-sitters become amateur sleuths, several small towns are ripped apart by scandal, and long-buried crimes come rising to the surface. All blurbs are my own, unless otherwise stated. Enjoy!

Wrobel’s latest is her best yet, chock-full of twists, turns, and unexpected reversals, with a perfectly constructed plot that’s also a rollicking good satire on the publishing industry. Revenge Plot certainly earns its comparisons to Misery, a loving homage that also manages to be entirely surprising. Here’s the gist: a desperate writer, young child in tow, shows up at a famous editor’s winter retreat and holds her hostage whilst making her read his manuscript (a fantasy shared by many writerly hopefuls, surely). As is true of all the best psychological thrillers, a series of well-paced reveals allows for constant reassessment of Wrobel’s characters and their unfortunate predicament, all leading up to the shocking denouement.

Collom won me as a fan with her excellent tale of feminist vengeance, Peter Miles Must Die, and her new book seems tailor-made for this Austin-based cat-loving crime editor. In Terrarium, a desperate barista and wannabe veterinarian finds her cat-sitting side gigs drying up after an unfortunate incident; a new job taking care of a wealthy artist’s big fluffy baby drops into her lap at the best possible time, but could her new employer be the Rainey Street killer? Fingers crossed!

May Cobb, All the Little Secrets (Sourcebooks Landmark)

May Cobb’s follow-up to All the Little Houses functions well as both sequel and standalone—it’s set in the same small town, and begins just after the concluding events of previous volume, but with plenty of new momentum and diverging narratives. All the Little Secrets is not so much about a crime, as about the fallout from a crime—these pages feature attempted coverups, scandalous reveals, and quite a few zingers, for a highly entertaining work harking back to the stylized dramatics of 1980s soap operas.

Carter Wilson, When They Find Me (Poisoned Pen)

This is a genuine pulse pounder, about two women, both on the run (in a manner of speaking), whose families and destinies collide in the freezing, remote Colorado wilderness. Annie has been living off the grid in a small cabin with her daughter Coral, hiding out, hoping the people hunting her never find her again. And Riven, who has spent her life living off the land, away from society, arrives at this strange town thanks to her father, who swears revenge on the man who ruined his life. –OR

Gabrielle Lucille Fuentes, Clear Water (Flatiron)

Gabrielle Lucille Fuentes’s new novel is eerie, atmospheric, and highly compelling. Listen to this premise, and tell me you’re not interested right away? When Alma encounters a girl in white she’s never seen before, she’s not sure what she’s seeing—is this person a vision, a ghost of one of the many girls who have vanished in her small Wisconsin town? Some people say that the girls are dangerous, others are too afraid to even wonder. But Alma, who lost her own sister in high school, is determined to find an explanation for the strange events in the town, once and for all. –OR

Molly Odintz is the managing editor for CrimeReads and the editor of Austin Noir, now available from Akashic Books. She grew up in Austin and worked as a bookseller before becoming a Very Professional Internet Person. She lives in central Texas with her cat, Fritz Lang.

#7560. Agency Annual FOIA Reports Due on Agency Websites

#7559. Justice Department Announces Actions to Disrupt North Korea’s Massive Theft of Cryptocurrency

An official website of the United States government

Official websites use .gov A .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPS A lock ( Lock Locked padlock ) or https:// means you’ve safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

Today the Department of Justice announced coordinated actions against the Democratic People’s Republic of North Korea (DPRK) government’s schemes to fund its regime through the massive theft of cryptocurrency. These actions include the seizure of cloud-based infrastructure used in the FaceStealer social engineering scheme that has resulted in the theft of more than $400 million worth of cryptocurrency and the court-authorized seizure and restraint of more than $1.2 billion worth of cryptocurrency recently stolen from Binance, a virtual currency exchange.

“North Korea’s government bankrolls its rogue regime and illicit weapons program through cryptocurrency theft,” said Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General for National Security M. Scott Proctor. “Today’s announcement shows that America will fight back against that corrupt regime’s effort to profit through cybercrime. The National Security Division will use every tool at its disposal to seize and return North Korea’s ill-gotten gains.”

“The Criminal Division, specifically the Computer Crime and Intellectual Property Section (CCIPS), and our law enforcement partners played the critical role in immediately freezing billions of dollars’ worth of stolen Binance digital assets before they got beyond law enforcement reach,” said Assistant Attorney General A. Tysen Duva of the Justice Department’s Criminal Division. “As the Department’s liaison to the digital asset industry, the Criminal Division quickly and efficiently used its expertise to immediately halt about half of this historic breach.”

“Cryptocurrency unfortunately has become fertile ground for North Korea to defraud victims and fund its malign activities,” said First Assistant U.S. Attorney Bill Essayli for the Central District of California. “Today’s seizure shows that America will continue the fight against rogue states who financially gain from cybercrime.”

“By springing into action at a moment’s notice, we worked together to freeze this massive criminal fraud in its tracks,” said U.S. Attorney Jeanine Ferris Pirro for the District of Columbia. “The North Korean regime may think they are sophisticated cyber criminals, but we have consistently met them head on, as we did here seizing more than $1.2 billion of their stolen assets.”

“The North Korean regime relies on proceeds from large-scale cryptocurrency theft to fund its illicit weapons program, stealing billions of dollars in recent years,” said FBI Cyber Branch Operations Director Brett Leatherman. “North Korean cyber actors have used the FaceStealer scheme to aggressively target individuals in the cryptocurrency and Web3 sectors through deceptive social engineering tactics, resulting in the theft of more than $400 million in the last 18 months. The FBI remains committed to identifying, disrupting, and imposing cost on North Korean actors targeting the virtual asset community.”

“North Korea’s FaceStealer is a highly effective social engineering scheme capable of deceiving even the most experienced technical and security conscious computer user” said Assistant Director in Charge Patrick Grandy of the FBI’s Los Angeles Field Office. “It exploits our trust in ubiquitous video conference platforms and valued personal relationships. If you can’t imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough. The FBI is sounding the alarm to elevate awareness of this growing scheme, now aimed not only at crypto theft, but also the exploitation of the of the open-source software supply chain.”

Seizure of FaceStealer Infrastructure – Central District of California

Today, the U.S. Attorney’s Office for the Central District of California announced the disruption of cloud-based infrastructure used by North Korean hackers in a highly effective social engineering scheme that the FBI is calling “FaceStealer.” The scheme, which relies on surreptitiously recorded videos and artificial deepfakes, has resulted in the theft of more than $400 million in the last 18 months and the attempted compromise of individuals involved in the open-source software supply chain.

North Korean hackers known publicly by the names Sapphire Sleet, UNC1069, Dangerous Password, CryptoCore, Advanced Persistent Threat 38 (APT38), BlueNoroff, and Stardust Chollima have lured targets with invitations to video meetings delivered from accounts stolen from prior victims. Once a target joins the meeting, the hackers record the target’s webcam video and play a prerecorded video of a previous victim or victims known to the target, thereby simulating the participation of trusted contacts in the meeting to gain the target’s trust. The North Korean hackers also use super intelligence (SI) to develop deepfake videos of well-known and influential individuals often in the cryptocurrency and web3 industries that make the FaceStealer scheme even more effective. Here again, they use the deepfake videos in the meetings with targets to gain the target’s trust.

During the meeting, the hackers then introduce a fake technical error into the meeting, such as claiming that the target cannot be heard, and provide a link or prompt to fix the error. The “fix” surreptitiously installs malware on the target’s computer. With the target’s computer compromised, the hackers can steal cryptocurrency or exploit the target’s access to other computer infrastructure. In several reported cases, the hackers stole cryptocurrency and hijacked communication accounts several months to more than a year after the initial compromise. As part of the scheme the hackers also accessed the new victim’s contact lists, social media, and communications apps to target the victim’s personal and professional contacts.

The North Korean hackers target individuals working in the cryptocurrency and web3 industries. Additionally, they have increasingly targeted individuals involved in the development and maintenance of open-source software with the FaceStealer technique, attempting to gain access to their devices and the software repository accounts they control. This targeting appears calculated to enable software supply-chain attacks. Pursuant to a federal warrant, the FBI seized over 900 webcam videos of FaceStealer victims.

The FBI today published a report providing greater detail on the mechanics of the FaceStealer scheme, including best practices to protect against this scheme and steps victims can take in the event of a compromise. Additionally, the FBI created a website, www.fbi.gov/facestealer, to serve as a centralized hub for resources and information on the scheme.

The effort to disrupt the FaceStealer campaign was led by Assistant U.S. Attorney Khaldoun Shobaki for the Central District of California and the FBI’s Los Angeles Field Office.

The Department, FBI, and Law Enforcement Partners Interdict More Than a Billion Dollars Worth of Virtual Assets Stolen From Binance

Separately from the FaceStealer campaign, on or about Oct. 29, members of the North Korean military, referred to by the FBI as “TraderTraitor” stole more than $6 billion worth of virtual assets from the Binance cryptocurrency exchange. Since the theft, the National Security Division’s National Security Cyber Section, the Criminal Division’s Computer Crime and Intellectual Property Section, the U.S. Attorney’s Office for the District of Columbia, and law enforcement partners have worked closely with virtual asset service providers (VASPs) to interdict the stolen assets. Collective U.S. government and private sector efforts so far have resulted in the freeze of more than $2.7 billion, with the Department having already obtained court-authorization to seize or restrain in excess of $1.2 billion of that amount.

Efforts to trace, freeze, seize, and forfeit the stolen virtual assets are ongoing, as TraderTraitor actors continue to launder such assets through various cryptocurrency bridges, mixers, and exchanges. For example, last week, the FBI, in coordination with its law enforcement partners at the U.S. Secret Service (USSS) and the Internal Revenue Service (IRS-CI), issued a Cyber Alert attributing the theft and urging VASPs to block transactions with or derived from cryptocurrency addresses TraderTraitor actors are using to launder the stolen Binance assets.

The FBI’s Los Angeles Field Office, Cyber Division, and Virtual Asset Unit are coordinating the FBI’s efforts to recover the stolen virtual assets, working in partnership with the USSS and the IRS-CI.

Alexander Scott Mercurio, 21, of Coeur d’Alene, Idaho, was sentenced today to 18 years in prison and lifetime supervised release by U.S. District Judge David C. Nye of the District...

#7556. But is it a crime to fight a bear?

Adult, male black bears can run faster than Usain Bolt. Thirty five miles per hour. No sweat. They can sprint and jump and climb full speed up a one hundred foot tree in a matter of seconds. They can flip three hundred pound rocks with one forepaw and drag thousand pound carcasses with ease. Their muscle fibers are packed so tightly and can generate enough raw horsepower that they can tear open car doors like we’d open a refrigerator. They’re six feet tall standing on their back legs and they’ve got two inch claws built for digging and climbing or slashing. The average man would have to be insane to try and fight one in the real world. But in the world of fiction, this idea makes one hell of a fun premise to write about and during my research it brought up an interesting question:

Fighting a bear is crazy, sure, but is it a crime?

First, Brick Bridger, our hero in Bear Brawl, is not your average man. He’s 6’6” tall and weighs 265 pounds. He’s a two-time collegiate wrestling champion and had eight wins in the UFC before he retired. And he’s properly motivated. A rogue, 450-pound black bear killed his mom and put his brother in a wheelchair.

The book explores what would happen if a man such as this dedicated his life to training, tracking, finding and fighting this bear with no weapons to get revenge. He enlists a zoologist, his ex-wrestling coach, local trackers and hunters and learns everything he can about this bear to prepare for the battle.

But unlike, say, Chief Martin Brody, in Jaws, who heads out into the ocean as a law enforcement officer to hunt the great white shark terrorizing his beaches, Brick is just a citizen. Brody puts a bounty on this one specific shark, overriding the laws that make killing, catching or removing a great white shark from the ocean a crime. Brick can’t do that.

Now, you might be thinking, “there are hunting licenses issued for black bears in many states” and you’d be correct. Hunters have been killing bears for their meat and fur for centuries. Just read Craig Fehrman’s excellent recent book, This Vast Enterprise, and the number of bears killed and eaten during the Lewis and Clark expedition will astonish you.

Still, hunting with a rifle and a license is a far cry from walking into the woods to fight a bear unarmed and as of this writing, there are no states that issue a bear fighting permit that would allow someone to do this. Interestingly enough, there are states that specifically ban wrestling bears: Arkansas, Louisiana, Oklahoma and Mississippi.

This mostly refers to carnivals and expeditions and circus-like events being banned, because it isn’t as if there is a queue of men applying to wrestle bears for amusement in the wild. On this front, in those states, if you were caught wrestling a bear, yes, it would be a crime.

In Arkansas, where Bear Brawl takes place, AR Code § 5-62-124, considers wrestling a bear for “fun” or money “unlawful bear exploitation” and it is a Class D felony.”

So, did my main character commit a felony by fighting a rampaging black bear that had killed four people?

Not exactly, because there is a self-defense clause. Arkansas Game and Fish Commission Code 01.00-G puts the burden on the person who killed the bear to prove they acted under a “good faith belief” that they were protecting themselves or someone else from “imminent bodily harm or serious injury.”

This one’s dicey for our hero, Brick, because he specifically went into the woods to find and fight the bear. Fortunately for him, this particular bear was in the middle of a rampage and had just attacked a grandfather, grandmother and their grandson, which puts Brick on the right side of the law.

Okay, so now we know that the actual bear brawl in Bear Brawl is legal. Whew. The only caveat is that if Brick wins the fight (no spoilers) he better not be tempted to take a claw or a tooth as a prize. Why? Because even if you’re justified in a bear killing in Arkansas, as soon as you take anything from the carcass, you’ve gone from being a life-saving hero to an illegal poacher in no time flat, and now you’ve committed a crime again. Ha!

You’ll just have to read my thriller Bear Brawl to discover how this whole thing shakes out. Man vs. bear. No guns. No weapons. Just revenge. Part Jaws and part Rocky, it’s an action thriller that reads like your favorite blockbuster movie. And no animal fighting laws were broken while writing the book. I promise.

Jon Finkel is the award-winning author of The Life of Dad, The Athlete, Heart Over Height, "Mean" Joe Greene, the Greatest Stars of the NBA series, and other books about sports, fatherhood, fitness, and more. His work has been endorsed by Spike Lee, Tony Dungy, Jerry Jones, Mark Cuban, and Chef Robert Irvine. He is cohost of the Life of Dad Sports Show podcast and Lunch Break Facebook Live show with more than one thousand viewers. He has written for GQ, Men's Health, Yahoo! Sports, the New York Times, and dozens of other national publications. He lives in Royal Palm Beach, Florida.

#7554. Justice Thomas will teach seminar on race, equality at University of Texas at Austin

U.S. Supreme Court Justice Clarence Thomas answers questions during a visit to the University of Texas at Austin on April 15, 2026. (Photo by Eric Gay/The Associated Press)

U.S. Supreme Court Justice Clarence Thomas will lead an undergraduate seminar at the University of Texas at Austin on race, equality and political thought in February.

The one-credit-hour seminar, “CIV 166 Equality and the New Birth of Freedom,” will include two hours of lecture daily from Feb. 1 to Feb. 5, 2027, for 17 students. Sixty-five applied.

Students will also meet with Justin Dyer, the dean of the University of Texas at Austin’s School of Civic Leadership, during the seminar, which follows American political thought from the nation’s founding through the Civil War to the present.

Readings include the writings and speeches of Frederick Douglass, Booker T. Washington and W.E.B. Du Bois, as well as civil rights leaders the Rev. Martin Luther King Jr. and Malcolm X. The course examines how questions of race and equality show up in recent Supreme Court decisions.

Thomas previously spoke to students and legal professionals at the University of Texas at Austin in April, making the case that the Supreme Court’s failures have stemmed from a lack of courage, not a lack of understanding, and he discussed Plessy v. Ferguson, a 1896 ruling that upheld government-enforced segregation.

Thomas, who joined the Supreme Court in 1991, is its longest-serving current member.

#7553. The Next Film From Disney and Pixar is a Crime Movie set in Italy… with Cats

Listen up, wiseguys. There’s a new Disney/Pixar movie coming out, and it’s a crime movie! Specifically, it’s a movie about the Italian mob. It takes place in Venice, Italy, and is the story of a scrawny, impoverished young fellow named Nero (voiced by Mark Ruffalo) who is taken in by a powerful crime family. In exchange for this, his job is to steal things for them… often at great personal risk.

Also, they’re all cats. Everybody’s a cat. Well, a couple of characters seem to be rodents. And there’s a pigeon, I think. But it’s mostly cats. The film is titled Gatto (which is Italian for “cat”).

Anyway, Nero starts to find himself in very dangerous situations, working as a thief for the (literal) fat cat crime boss Rocco, and learns that he has nine lives that he’s wasting, dying while doing criminal errands. Thus, he asks himself the great questions many crime movie protagonists ask themselves: is everything he’s doing worth it? Is he living a life that’s worth living as if it’s his only one? How can he change his life so that it’s full of meaning?

In addition to Ruffalo, the voice cast includes: Laurence Fishburne, Enrico Casarosa (who also directs), John Turturro, Isabella Rossellini, Natasia Demetriou, Jamie Demetriou, Jason Schwartzman, Saverio Raimondo, Jason Mantzoukas, Tracey Ullman, Marco Calvani, and Rhys Darby.

Perhaps it’s a great film to take your children to as you start to introduce them to the “mafia movie” genre. Watch Gatto and then The Godfather and find out which one they like better!

The film will be in theaters next March. Ci vediamo!

#7544. Wells Fargo faces labor board complaint over union vote

The National Labor Relations Board alleged the lender threatened and retaliated against workers in favor of unionization at a California branch.

The National Labor Relations Board has accused Wells Fargo of illegally threatening and retaliating against employees and coercing workers to prevent fair unionization votes at a California branch.

Most employees of an Atwater, California, branch who signed cards supporting unionization in December 2023 voted against unionization the following month, a regional director of the NLRB alleged, in a complaint filed Feb. 11 and seen by Bloomberg,

The NLRB claims Wells Fargo coerced workers to vote against unionization. Wells Fargo has denied that allegation.

The labor board wants the bank to recognize and negotiate with the union despite the vote, based on a 2023 precedent case. The 2023 Cemex ruling strengthened the NLRB's power to compel companies that violate labor laws to recognize unions.

However, Republicans and business groups oppose the precedent. The acting general counsel of NLRB, William Cowen, or his permanent replacement, could withdraw, amend, or settle the Wells Fargo complaint.

President Donald Trump appointed Cowen acting general counsel this month, days after firing NLRB’s previous chief lawyer Jennifer Abruzzo, a Biden appointee, and Democratic board member Gwynne Wilcox.

The email notifying Wilcox of her termination said she was being fired since she did not “share the objectives” of the present administration. Wilcox’s ouster has left the board with only two members coupled with existing vacancies and does not meet the minimum number of members needed to adjudicate routine cases.

Wilcox is suing Trump, alleging the president surpassed his authority in firing her and requesting that she be allowed to resume her work as an NLRB member.

The NLRB complaint does not signify a complete investigation or a full adjudication of the allegations; rather, the regional office found that the allegations may be sufficient to go to a hearing to consider all evidence and make credibility determinations, a Wells Fargo spokesperson noted.

Employees at the Atwater branch voted 3-1 to reject unionization in January 2024. The branch manager allegedly told staff she knew about organizing discussions and warned about camera surveillance. She also reportedly told workers that Wells Fargo would apply stricter work conditions and would make union supporters stay longer hours.

A Wells Fargo spokesperson said the bank trains its managers to respect the rights of its employees to freely associate and join or support a union, or to bargain collectively with a representative of their choosing.

“We train our managers to comply with labor law and respect the rights of our employees,” the spokesperson told Banking Dive via email. “In this case, the majority of eligible employees freely voted against a union in a Board supervised secret ballot election and the facts do not support allegations to the contrary.”

Wells Fargo is cooperating with the investigation but believes these allegations are unfounded and plans to defend the company and employees, the spokesperson added.

An NLRB hearing before a judge is scheduled for October, and Wells Fargo, if ordered to collectively bargain, can appeal to NLRB members in Washington, Bloomberg reported.

Recently, unionized Wells Fargo employees hit a new challenge in organizing. The bank's conduct management intake team won its union vote 21-16 in December, making it the first non-branch unit at Wells Fargo to unionize.

The team, which handles customer and employee complaints across various locations, has now filed an unfair labor practice charge with the NLRB through Wells Fargo Workers United-CWA. The complaint alleges that the bank illegally refuses to engage in collective bargaining following the certified union vote, American Banker reported. However, the bank contends it’s waiting for the outcome of the NLRB’s review and claims there were procedural flaws in the handling of the vote.

CWA unionized 24 Wells Fargo branches last year, with around 200 employees being organized.

Wells Fargo CEO Charlie Scharf pushed back on employee unionization efforts last April, saying it’s important to “make clear that we continue to believe that our employees are best served by working directly with Wells Fargo and our leadership team.”

#7542. Calling judge vulgar name doesn’t amount to criminal contempt, state supreme court says

Calling a judge a vulgar name is rude, but it does not amount to criminal contempt, the Pennsylvania Supreme Court recently found. (Image from Shutterstock)

The criminal contempt conviction of a courtroom spectator who called a judge a vulgar name after she ruled against a motion that his acquaintance filed was recently vacated by the Pennsylvania Supreme Court.

Basile Outlaw spent 30 days in jail after the 2022 incident involving Philadelphia County Court of Common Pleas Judge Roxanne E. Covington, Reason reports.

“What did you say?” Covington responded to his remark, according to the article.

He claims that he later came back to apologize and was taken into custody, Reason reports.

In December 2023, the Pennsylvania Superior Court upheld Outlaw’s conviction. The state supreme court reversed and vacated the conviction in late October, finding that while what Outlaw did was rude, it wasn’t enough for criminal contempt.

The majority opinion cited an earlier finding involving a pro se party in a custody dispute calling the judge a vulgar term.

Here, the Pennsylvania Supreme Court also reversed a criminal contempt conviction for the party. In both cases, the state supreme court found, the outbursts amounted to momentary breaks in the proceedings, not significant disruptions.

#7541. The SEC has requested to delay climate rule arguments. What’s next?

Sustainable investing experts are not surprised by the delay and say it is expected to have minimal immediate impact.

Acting Securities and Exchange Commission Chair Mark Uyeda’s decision last week to ask the Eighth Circuit Court of Appeals to delay arguments for the agency’s climate risk disclosure rule did not come as a surprise to regulatory experts in the space.

While the change is expected to have minimal immediate impact, it sets the stage for a shift in focus for the SEC under the Trump administration.

The SEC issued its long-awaited climate-risk disclosure rule last March, nearly two years after it was first proposed — and amid over 24,000 public comments. Uyeda was one of two commissioners to vote against the rule’s approval. The rule was immediately met with multiple lawsuits that were later consolidated into the Eighth Circuit. The spate of legal challenges led the agency to stay the rule’s implementation in April.

Given the agency’s stay and the change in administration, Tom Willman, regulatory lead for artificial intelligence sustainability platform Clarity AI, said Uyeda’s decision came “as no great surprise.”

“It is nevertheless disappointing that investors will be deprived of much-needed financially material information related to climate related risks,” Willman said by email Wednesday.

Julie Gorte, senior vice president of sustainable investing at Impax Asset Management was more blunt in an interview: “The only person on the planet who didn't see this coming was Rip Van Winkle,” she said Wednesday.

Uyeda’s recommended pullback on defending the SEC climate rule follows his prior critiques of the rule. During the hearing on the final climate rule, in the weeks after it was finalized and in his Feb. 11 statement announcing the delay request, Uyeda maintained his belief that the SEC does not have the authority to promulgate the rule or require such disclosures.

Jenner & Block Partner Jennifer Lee, a former assistant director of the SEC Enforcement Division, said Uyeda’s position is “consistent with the new, Republican-led SEC’s shift back to focusing on ‘bread and butter’ investor disclosure issues.” Lee, who now represents companies and financial institutions in regulatory investigations and litigation, told ESG Dive in emailed comments Thursday that she expects that focus to include an emphasis on materiality, while looking to avoid “overly prescriptive disclosure requirements for public companies.”

The change in SEC posture, however, is expected to have a minimal impact for companies or investors, according to Willman and Gorte.

“The impact of this is going to be extraordinarily minimal, partly because it was never enforced in the first place,” Gorte said Wednesday. “So we never had better climate reporting stimulated by U.S. regulatory postures to begin with. And for U.S. companies, or for any company really, a lot of the big ones are probably still going to have to be reporting on all the same things that were in that rule because of [other jurisdictions].”

Willman echoed that note and said that many U.S. companies already voluntarily report climate risks due to investor demand, in addition to growing regulatory requirements both overseas in jurisdictions like the European Union, and stateside in California. New York recently reintroduced its own version of California’s climate disclosure laws.

Additionally, Stephen O’Day, a partner at Smith, Gambrell & Russell, noted the intervenors in the case could continue the rule’s defense, even though the SEC’s announcement indicates this iteration of the agency is not inclined to defend the rules. In the case of the climate rule, 18 states and the District of Columbia have signed on as intervenors to support the agency’s rule.

O’Day said a similar process happened after intervenors continued the defense for enforcement actions taken by the Clinton-era administration for violations of the Clean Air Act by coal-fired plants. Ultimately, the intervenors prevailed, and the Supreme Court upheld the actions.

“The proceedings pending in the Eighth Circuit involve challenges filed by multiple parties and intervenors, any of which could seek to continue defense of the SEC rules,” O’Day said. “So, while the court may grant the requested 45-day pause, it remains to be seen whether any such pause will result in the end of the SEC’s final rules.”

Regardless, sustainability programs and disclosures are expected to continue.

“Regulatory shifts don’t change the fundamental reality: physical climate risk is already priced into markets,” Lukky Ahmed, CEO of climate risk data and analytics platform Climate X, said by email Wednesday. Firms have a fiduciary responsibility to continue to assess, manage and disclose climate risks to their operations, Ahmed said.

“Integrating climate resilience and adaptation strategies isn’t just prudent — it’s essential for maintaining business performance, driving shareholder returns, and protecting communities," Ahmed said.

Despite the shifting federal policy stance, “there is a lot of faith that the drivers of sustainability remain in place no matter who is in anyone's White House,” Gorte told ESG Dive.

“If we don't solve the climate crisis, there are going to be … parts of the globe that are probably uninhabitable, or where people can't work outdoors in the summer. If we don't solve the biodiversity crisis, the prospects are even worse.” Gorte said. “So there are things that we have to do in order to preserve what we think of as life as we know it, and those drivers are going to remain in place.”
